========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it's nice to meet you too, Doctor. I... I guess I came in because I keep doing these odd things at church lately. 

Like, we have a potluck on Sundays. Sister Mary always brings her macaroni salad, though she puts way too much celery in it if you ask me. I used to grow celery when we had the house out on County Road 9, but the soil out there was always too sandy for a good garden. The tomatoes did alright, though. 

Anyway, I was standing by the potluck table, and I picked up the big serving spoon for the potatoes, and I just... held it up in the air. I just stood there holding it up and not moving at all. Just froze right there. 

And then later, I was at the end of the table with the napkins. They were those yellow paper ones. Yellow is such a happy color, it reminds me of the kitchen curtains I bought at Sears back when they still mailed out that big thick catalog... I really miss getting that catalog in the mail. But I was folding the same stack of napkins, just folding them over and over, smoothing them down and folding them again, and all those people were just standing there in line waiting on me to finish so they could get their silverware. I don't really know why I get stuck like that.

Harm me? Oh, heavens no. Why would anyone want to do that? I keep to myself mostly, just me and my little garden out back. I've been trying to get the tomatoes to come in, but the squirrels keep getting to them before they turn red. They're such clever little things, squirrels. That tree must have been a hundred years old. My brother built a treehouse up in the branches. We'd sit up there for hours and eat sandwiches... egg salad, usually. Mother always put a little bit of paprika in her egg salad, gave it such a nice color. I still have her old recipe box, it's made of tin with little painted daisies on the lid. But no, nobody's trying to control me or anything like that. I'm just fine.

It would fill up the whole porch. Smelled a bit like cherry pie, actually. I love a good cherry pie, especially if you make the crust from scratch and do the lattice on top. They're such clever little things, squirrels. They have those bushy tails that twitch when they're excited. I used to watch them chase each other up and down the big oak tree we had in the front yard when I was a girl. That tree must have been a hundred years old. My brother built a treehouse up in the branches. We'd sit up there for hours and eat sandwiches... egg salad, usually. Mother always put a little bit of paprika in her egg salad, gave it such a nice color. I still have her old recipe box, it's made of tin with little painted daisies on the lid. Repeating words? No, I don't think I do that. It sounds a bit like an echo, doesn't it? Like when you stand at the edge of a canyon and shout down into it. We went to the Grand Canyon once, Henry and I, for our twenty-fifth anniversary. It was so hot that day, the sun just beating down on the rocks. The rocks were all these different shades of red and orange, like a painting. I bought a little postcard from the gift shop with a picture of a mule on it. They take people down into the canyon on mules, you know. I'd never ride one, though. Mules can be so stubborn. My uncle Arthur had a mule on his farm back when I was little. He called him Barnaby. Barnaby loved to eat carrots right out of your hand, but you had to keep your fingers flat or he'd nip you. I was always so careful with my hands around him... But no, I don't go around repeating what people say.

I keep to myself mostly, just me and my little garden out back. I've been trying to get the tomatoes to come in, but the squirrels keep getting to them before they turn red. They're such clever little things, squirrels. They have those bushy tails that twitch when they're excited. I used to watch them chase each other up and down the big oak tree we had in the front yard when I was a girl. That tree must have been a hundred years old. My brother built a treehouse up in the branches. We'd sit up there for hours and eat sandwiches... egg salad, usually. Mother always put a little bit of paprika in her egg salad, gave it such a nice color. I still have her old recipe box, it's made of tin with little painted daisies on the lid. But no, nobody's trying to control me or anything like that. I'm just fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeating other people’s words right after they say them?\n\nPatient: Repeating words? No, I don't think I do that. It sounds a bit like an echo, doesn't it? Like when you stand at the edge of a canyon and shout down into it. We went to the Grand Canyon once, Henry and I, for our twenty-fifth anniversary. It was so hot that day, the sun just beating down on the rocks. The rocks were all these different shades of red and orange, like a painting. I bought a little postcard from the gift shop with a picture of a mule on it. They take people down into the canyon on mules, you know. I'd never ride one, though. Mules can be so stubborn. My uncle Arthur had a mule on his farm back when I was little. He called him Barnaby. Barnaby loved to eat carrots right out of your hand, but you had to keep your fingers flat or he'd nip you. I was always so careful with my hands around him... But no, I don't go around repeating what people say.\n\n[Last Question]\nAs a child, did you often do the same movement or routine over and over again?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Over and over? No, I don't recall doing anything like that. I was just a regular little girl, mostly just running around outside. We did play jump rope a lot, I suppose, if that counts? You have to jump the exact same way every time or the rope catches your ankles. My friend Martha and I would jump rope for hours on the sidewalk until the streetlights came on. Martha had the most beautiful blonde hair, always tied up in two braids with little red ribbons. Her mother made those ribbons from scraps of velvet. Velvet is such a difficult fabric to sew, you know. It slips all over the machine if you aren't careful. I tried making a velvet dress for my baby doll once, but the needle kept jamming. It was a Singer sewing machine, one of those heavy black iron ones with the gold filigree painted on the side. My mother bought it second-hand from a lady down the street who was moving up to Chicago. Chicago always sounded so windy and cold to me, all those tall buildings blocking out the sun... I've never been, myself. But no, no strange routines or movements for me. Just regular playing.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, it's nice to meet you too, Doctor. I... I guess I came in because I keep doing these odd things at church lately. \n\nLike, we have a potluck on Sundays. Sister Mary always brings her macaroni salad, though she puts way too much celery in it if you ask me. I used to grow celery when we had the house out on County Road 9, but the soil out there was always too sandy for a good garden. The tomatoes did alright, though. \n\nAnyway, I was standing by the potluck table, and I picked up the big serving spoon for the potatoes, and I just... held it up in the air. I just stood there holding it up and not moving at all. Just froze right there. \n\nAnd then later, I was at the end of the table with the napkins. They were those yellow paper ones. Yellow is such a happy color, it reminds me of the kitchen curtains I bought at Sears back when they still mailed out that big thick catalog... I really miss getting that catalog in the mail. But I was folding the same stack of napkins, just folding them over and over, smoothing them down and folding them again, and all those people were just standing there in line waiting on me to finish so they could get their silverware. I don't really know why I get stuck like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear voices when no one else is there?\n\nPatient: Voices? No, no, I don't hear anything like that. Just the regular sounds, you know? Like the wind chimes on my porch. I have these beautiful glass ones my sister got me when she went down to Florida. Clearwater, I think it was. Or maybe Daytona? She always loved the beach, but I could never stand the sand getting in my shoes. It just ruins a good pair of loafers. I used to have a pair of penny loafers that I wore until the soles practically fell off. They were so comfortable, like walking on clouds. But no... no voices. Just the wind chimes.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times lately when you felt unusually energetic or excited for days at a time?\n\nPatient: Energetic? Oh, heavens no. I don't think I've had that kind of energy since... well, since I was a girl. I remember one summer, I must have been about twelve, and we were staying at my aunt's farm. Pennies used to buy you a whole handful of candy down at the mercantile. Peppermint sticks, licorice whips... I always liked the butterscotch discs the best. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Frozen? Oh, goodness, no. I don't freeze up like a statue or anything. And who would be trying to move my arms anyway? It's just me here. The only thing frozen around here is the ground beef I put in the icebox. I always buy the big family pack when it goes on sale at the market. You have to remember to separate it into little portions before you freeze it, though, or it takes absolutely forever to thaw out on the counter. My neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, she just throws the whole big block right into her freezer, paper and all. Then she complains when she can't get her meatloaf made in time for supper. She makes a terribly dry meatloaf anyway, puts way too much onion in it. Onions give me such awful heartburn. I always have to drink a little glass of milk with a spoonful of baking soda stirred in if I have onions. My grandmother taught me that trick. She lived to be ninety-two, bless her heart, and she swore by baking soda for just about everything... But no, I move my own arms just fine. Nobody is posing me like a mannequin.

I used to grow celery when we had the house out on County Road 9, but the soil out there was always too sandy for a good garden. The tomatoes did alright, though. \n\nAnyway, I was standing by the potluck table, and I picked up the big serving spoon for the potatoes, and I just... held it up in the air. I just stood there holding it up and not moving at all. Just froze right there. \n\nAnd then later, I was at the end of the table with the napkins. They were those yellow paper ones. Yellow is such a happy color, it reminds me of the kitchen curtains I bought at Sears back when they still mailed out that big thick catalog... I really miss getting that catalog in the mail. But I was folding the same stack of napkins, just folding them over and over, smoothing them down and folding them again, and all those people were just standing there in line waiting on me to finish so they could get their silverware. I don't really know why I get stuck like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear voices when no one else is there?\n\nPatient: Voices? No, no, I don't hear anything like that. Just the regular sounds, you know? Like the wind chimes on my porch. I have these beautiful glass ones my sister got me when she went down to Florida. Clearwater, I think it was. Or maybe Daytona? She always loved the beach, but I could never stand the sand getting in my shoes. It just ruins a good pair of loafers. I used to have a pair of penny loafers that I wore until the soles practically fell off. They were so comfortable, like walking on clouds. But no... no voices. Just the wind chimes.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times lately when you felt unusually energetic or excited for days at a time?\n\nPatient: Energetic? Oh, heavens no. I don't think I've had that kind of energy since... well, since I was a girl. I remember one summer, I must have been about twelve, and we were staying at my aunt's farm. She had these big, beautiful draft horses. Clydesdales, they were. Such gentle giants, you know? They had those big feathery hooves. I used to brush their coats until they shone like copper pennies. Pennies used to buy you a whole handful of candy down at the mercantile. Peppermint sticks, licorice whips... I always liked the butterscotch discs the best. They came in those little shiny yellow wrappers. So pretty. But no, I wouldn't say I've felt unusually excited or anything like that lately. Just my normal self, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Since you were a child, have social situations usually felt confusing to you?\n\nPatient: Confusing? Oh, I don't know... I've always just been me, you know? I remember when I first started school, it was a little one-room schoolhouse out past the old mill. The teacher, Miss Gable, she had the most beautiful handwriting. Perfect cursive, all those loops and swirls. We used to practice on these little slate boards with chalk that always made my hands feel so dry. I hate that feeling, dry hands. I use this rosewater lotion now, comes in a pink bottle. Smells just like the roses my grandmother grew on her trellis. They were those big climbing roses, the kind that bloom all summer long... But no, I wouldn't say confusing. My head is perfectly fine. I haven't been sick or in a hospital since... well, since my Henry broke his collarbone. That was years and years ago. He was up fixing the roof on that old shed out back. It had those green shingles, you know? The kind that get all thick with moss if you don't scrape them down in the spring. I told him to hire someone to do it, but he was always so stubborn about fixing things himself. Just like his father. His father used to smoke this pipe that smelled just like sweet cherry wood. But no, nobody's trying to control me or anything like that. I'm just fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeating other people’s words right after they say them?\n\nPatient: Repeating words? No, I don't think I do that. It sounds a bit like an echo, doesn't it? Like when you stand at the edge of a canyon and shout down into it. We went to the Grand Canyon once, Henry and I, for our twenty-fifth anniversary. It was so hot that day, the sun just beating down on the rocks. The rocks were all these different shades of red and orange, like a painting. I bought a little postcard from the gift shop with a picture of a mule on it. They take people down into the canyon on mules, you know. I'd never ride one, though. Mules can be so stubborn. My uncle Arthur had a mule on his farm back when I was little. He called him Barnaby. Barnaby loved to eat carrots right out of your hand, but you had to keep your fingers flat or he'd nip you. I was always so careful with my hands around him... But no, I don't go around repeating what people say.\n\nDoctor: As a child, did you often do the same movement or routine over and over again?\n\nPatient: Over and over? No, I don't recall doing anything like that. I was just a regular little girl, mostly just running around outside. We did play jump rope a lot, I suppose, if that counts? You have to jump the exact same way every time or the rope catches your ankles. My friend Martha and I would jump rope for hours on the sidewalk until the streetlights came on. Martha had the most beautiful blonde hair, always tied up in two braids with little red ribbons. Her mother made those ribbons from scraps of velvet. Velvet is such a difficult fabric to sew, you know. It slips all over the machine if you aren't careful. I tried making a velvet dress for my baby doll once, but the needle kept jamming. It was a Singer sewing machine, one of those heavy black iron ones with the gold filigree painted on the side. My mother bought it second-hand from a lady down the street who was moving up to Chicago. Chicago always sounded so windy and cold to me, all those tall buildings blocking out the sun... I've never been, myself. But no, no strange routines or movements for me. Just regular playing.\n\nDoctor: When someone tries to move your arms or body while you’re frozen, do you stay in that position?\n\nPatient: Frozen? Oh, goodness, no. I don't freeze up like a statue or anything. And who would be trying to move my arms anyway? It's just me here. The only thing frozen around here is the ground beef I put in the icebox. I always buy the big family pack when it goes on sale at the market. You have to remember to separate it into little portions before you freeze it, though, or it takes absolutely forever to thaw out on the counter. My neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, she just throws the whole big block right into her freezer, paper and all. Then she complains when she can't get her meatloaf made in time for supper. She makes a terribly dry meatloaf anyway, puts way too much onion in it. Onions give me such awful heartburn. I always have to drink a little glass of milk with a spoonful of baking soda stirred in if I have onions. My grandmother taught me that trick. She lived to be ninety-two, bless her heart, and she swore by baking soda for just about everything... But no, I move my own arms just fine. Nobody is posing me like a mannequin.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Stuck? Well, like I just said, I don't get frozen or stuck in the first place. And I certainly don't stop talking. My husband Henry, God rest his soul, he always used to say I could talk the ears off a brass monkey. He had all these funny little sayings like that. His father was in the Merchant Marines, you see, so Henry picked up all sorts of phrases from him. He had this beautiful old compass his father brought back from a trip... maybe it was Nova Scotia? It was solid brass and just so heavy. I used to have to polish it every Sunday before church with that stinky Brasso cream. It always left a terrible black smudge on my good dusting rags. I tried soaking them in bleach, but you have to be so careful with bleach or it eats right through the cotton. I ruined one of my favorite aprons that way, a lovely little yellow gingham one with pockets on the front. I used to keep my clothespins in those pockets when I hung the wash out on the line... But no, I don't stop talking or get stuck like you're saying.

Pennies used to buy you a whole handful of candy down at the mercantile. Peppermint sticks, licorice whips... I always liked the butterscotch discs the best. Yellow is such a happy color, it reminds me of the kitchen curtains I bought at Sears back when they still mailed out that big thick catalog... I really miss getting that catalog in the mail. But I was folding the same stack of napkins, just folding them over and over, smoothing them down and folding them again, and all those people were just standing there in line waiting on me to finish so they could get their silverware. I don't really know why I get stuck like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear voices when no one else is there?\n\nPatient: Voices? No, no, I don't hear anything like that. Just the regular sounds, you know? Like the wind chimes on my porch. I have these beautiful glass ones my sister got me when she went down to Florida. Clearwater, I think it was. Or maybe Daytona? She always loved the beach, but I could never stand the sand getting in my shoes. It just ruins a good pair of loafers. I used to have a pair of penny loafers that I wore until the soles practically fell off. They were so comfortable, like walking on clouds. But no... no voices. Just the wind chimes.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times lately when you felt unusually energetic or excited for days at a time?\n\nPatient: Energetic? Oh, heavens no. I don't think I've had that kind of energy since... well, since I was a girl. I remember one summer, I must have been about twelve, and we were staying at my aunt's farm. She had these big, beautiful draft horses. Clydesdales, they were. Such gentle giants, you know? They had those big feathery hooves. I used to brush their coats until they shone like copper pennies. Pennies used to buy you a whole handful of candy down at the mercantile. Peppermint sticks, licorice whips... I always liked the butterscotch discs the best. They came in those little shiny yellow wrappers. So pretty. But no, I wouldn't say I've felt unusually excited or anything like that lately. Just my normal self, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Since you were a child, have social situations usually felt confusing to you?\n\nPatient: Confusing? Oh, I don't know... I've always just been me, you know? I remember when I first started school, it was a little one-room schoolhouse out past the old mill. The teacher, Miss Gable, she had the most beautiful handwriting. Perfect cursive, all those loops and swirls. We used to practice on these little slate boards with chalk that always made my hands feel so dry. I hate that feeling, dry hands. I use this rosewater lotion now, comes in a pink bottle. Smells just like the roses my grandmother grew on her trellis. They were those big climbing roses, the kind that bloom all summer long... But no, I wouldn't say confusing. Just... normal, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent medical illness or brain problem, like a seizure, stroke, or head injury?\n\nPatient: A stroke or a head injury? Oh, goodness no. My head is perfectly fine. I haven't been sick or in a hospital since... well, since my Henry broke his collarbone. That was years and years ago. He was up fixing the roof on that old shed out back. It had those green shingles, you know? The kind that get all thick with moss if you don't scrape them down in the spring. I told him to hire someone to do it, but he was always so stubborn about fixing things himself. Just like his father. His father used to smoke this pipe that smelled just like sweet cherry wood. It would fill up the whole porch. Smelled a bit like cherry pie, actually. I love a good cherry pie, especially if you make the crust from scratch and do the lattice on top. You have to weave the dough strips just right or they sink right down into the fruit while it bakes... But no, no seizures or anything like that for me. Just healthy, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe anyone is trying to harm you or control you?\n\nPatient: Harm me? Oh, heavens no. Why would anyone want to do that? I keep to myself mostly, just me and my little garden out back. I've been trying to get the tomatoes to come in, but the squirrels keep getting to them before they turn red. They're such clever little things, squirrels. They have those bushy tails that twitch when they're excited. I used to watch them chase each other up and down the big oak tree we had in the front yard when I was a girl. That tree must have been a hundred years old. My brother built a treehouse up in the branches. We'd sit up there for hours and eat sandwiches... egg salad, usually. Mother always put a little bit of paprika in her egg salad, gave it such a nice color. I still have her old recipe box, it's made of tin with little painted daisies on the lid. But no, nobody's trying to control me or anything like that. I'm just fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeating other people’s words right after they say them?\n\nPatient: Repeating words? No, I don't think I do that. It sounds a bit like an echo, doesn't it? Like when you stand at the edge of a canyon and shout down into it. We went to the Grand Canyon once, Henry and I, for our twenty-fifth anniversary. It was so hot that day, the sun just beating down on the rocks. The rocks were all these different shades of red and orange, like a painting. I bought a little postcard from the gift shop with a picture of a mule on it. They take people down into the canyon on mules, you know. I'd never ride one, though. Mules can be so stubborn. My uncle Arthur had a mule on his farm back when I was little. He called him Barnaby. Barnaby loved to eat carrots right out of your hand, but you had to keep your fingers flat or he'd nip you. I was always so careful with my hands around him... But no, I don't go around repeating what people say.\n\nDoctor: As a child, did you often do the same movement or routine over and over again?\n\nPatient: Over and over? No, I don't recall doing anything like that. I was just a regular little girl, mostly just running around outside. We did play jump rope a lot, I suppose, if that counts? You have to jump the exact same way every time or the rope catches your ankles. My friend Martha and I would jump rope for hours on the sidewalk until the streetlights came on. Martha had the most beautiful blonde hair, always tied up in two braids with little red ribbons. Her mother made those ribbons from scraps of velvet. Velvet is such a difficult fabric to sew, you know. It slips all over the machine if you aren't careful. I tried making a velvet dress for my baby doll once, but the needle kept jamming. It was a Singer sewing machine, one of those heavy black iron ones with the gold filigree painted on the side. My mother bought it second-hand from a lady down the street who was moving up to Chicago. Chicago always sounded so windy and cold to me, all those tall buildings blocking out the sun... I've never been, myself. But no, no strange routines or movements for me. Just regular playing.\n\nDoctor: When someone tries to move your arms or body while you’re frozen, do you stay in that position?\n\nPatient: Frozen? Oh, goodness, no. I don't freeze up like a statue or anything. And who would be trying to move my arms anyway? It's just me here. The only thing frozen around here is the ground beef I put in the icebox. I always buy the big family pack when it goes on sale at the market. You have to remember to separate it into little portions before you freeze it, though, or it takes absolutely forever to thaw out on the counter. My neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, she just throws the whole big block right into her freezer, paper and all. Then she complains when she can't get her meatloaf made in time for supper. She makes a terribly dry meatloaf anyway, puts way too much onion in it. Onions give me such awful heartburn. I always have to drink a little glass of milk with a spoonful of baking soda stirred in if I have onions. My grandmother taught me that trick. She lived to be ninety-two, bless her heart, and she swore by baking soda for just about everything... But no, I move my own arms just fine. Nobody is posing me like a mannequin.\n\nDoctor: When you get stuck like that, do you stop talking too?\n\nPatient: Stuck? Well, like I just said, I don't get frozen or stuck in the first place. And I certainly don't stop talking. My husband Henry, God rest his soul, he always used to say I could talk the ears off a brass monkey. He had all these funny little sayings like that. His father was in the Merchant Marines, you see, so Henry picked up all sorts of phrases from him. He had this beautiful old compass his father brought back from a trip... maybe it was Nova Scotia? It was solid brass and just so heavy. I used to have to polish it every Sunday before church with that stinky Brasso cream. It always left a terrible black smudge on my good dusting rags. I tried soaking them in bleach, but you have to be so careful with bleach or it eats right through the cotton. I ruined one of my favorite aprons that way, a lovely little yellow gingham one with pockets on the front. I used to keep my clothespins in those pockets when I hung the wash out on the line... But no, I don't stop talking or get stuck like you're saying.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I suppose sometimes folks do look a bit confused when I'm talking. Like at the church, when the ladies ask me about the new blankets for the bazaar or what time choir practice is. I always mean to give them a straight answer, I really do. But then I start talking, and my words just sort of run off on their own... I'll start talking about the green beans I'm canning, and then that reminds me of a hymn we sang last Easter, and before I know it I'm going on about how my yellow roses are doing this year. And they just stare at me, like they can't quite find what I'm saying in all of that. My roses really are lovely this year, though. I put down some crushed eggshells around the roots, just like my mother used to do. She always saved her eggshells in a little tin cup on the windowsill. It was a blue tin cup, the kind that had those little white speckles all over it. We used to take those cups camping when I was a little girl... but yes, I suppose people do have a hard time following me sometimes.

No, I don't think I do that. It sounds a bit like an echo, doesn't it? Like when you stand at the edge of a canyon and shout down into it. We went to the Grand Canyon once, Henry and I, for our twenty-fifth anniversary. It was so hot that day, the sun just beating down on the rocks. The rocks were all these different shades of red and orange, like a painting. I bought a little postcard from the gift shop with a picture of a mule on it. They take people down into the canyon on mules, you know. I'd never ride one, though. Mules can be so stubborn. My uncle Arthur had a mule on his farm back when I was little. He called him Barnaby. Barnaby loved to eat carrots right out of your hand, but you had to keep your fingers flat or he'd nip you. I was always so careful with my hands around him... But no, I don't go around repeating what people say.\n\nDoctor: As a child, did you often do the same movement or routine over and over again?\n\nPatient: Over and over? No, I don't recall doing anything like that. I was just a regular little girl, mostly just running around outside. We did play jump rope a lot, I suppose, if that counts? You have to jump the exact same way every time or the rope catches your ankles. My friend Martha and I would jump rope for hours on the sidewalk until the streetlights came on. Martha had the most beautiful blonde hair, always tied up in two braids with little red ribbons. Her mother made those ribbons from scraps of velvet. Velvet is such a difficult fabric to sew, you know. It slips all over the machine if you aren't careful. I tried making a velvet dress for my baby doll once, but the needle kept jamming. It was a Singer sewing machine, one of those heavy black iron ones with the gold filigree painted on the side. My mother bought it second-hand from a lady down the street who was moving up to Chicago. Chicago always sounded so windy and cold to me, all those tall buildings blocking out the sun... I've never been, myself. But no, no strange routines or movements for me. Just regular playing.\n\nDoctor: When someone tries to move your arms or body while you’re frozen, do you stay in that position?\n\nPatient: Frozen? Oh, goodness, no. I don't freeze up like a statue or anything. And who would be trying to move my arms anyway? It's just me here. The only thing frozen around here is the ground beef I put in the icebox. I always buy the big family pack when it goes on sale at the market. You have to remember to separate it into little portions before you freeze it, though, or it takes absolutely forever to thaw out on the counter. My neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, she just throws the whole big block right into her freezer, paper and all. Then she complains when she can't get her meatloaf made in time for supper. She makes a terribly dry meatloaf anyway, puts way too much onion in it. Onions give me such awful heartburn. I always have to drink a little glass of milk with a spoonful of baking soda stirred in if I have onions. My grandmother taught me that trick. She lived to be ninety-two, bless her heart, and she swore by baking soda for just about everything... But no, I move my own arms just fine. Nobody is posing me like a mannequin.\n\nDoctor: When you get stuck like that, do you stop talking too?\n\nPatient: Stuck? Well, like I just said, I don't get frozen or stuck in the first place. And I certainly don't stop talking. My husband Henry, God rest his soul, he always used to say I could talk the ears off a brass monkey. He had all these funny little sayings like that. His father was in the Merchant Marines, you see, so Henry picked up all sorts of phrases from him. He had this beautiful old compass his father brought back from a trip... maybe it was Nova Scotia? It was solid brass and just so heavy. I used to have to polish it every Sunday before church with that stinky Brasso cream. It always left a terrible black smudge on my good dusting rags. I tried soaking them in bleach, but you have to be so careful with bleach or it eats right through the cotton. I ruined one of my favorite aprons that way, a lovely little yellow gingham one with pockets on the front. I used to keep my clothespins in those pockets when I hung the wash out on the line... But no, I don't stop talking or get stuck like you're saying.\n\nDoctor: Do people ever tell you that your talking jumps from one idea to another in a way that’s hard to follow?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose sometimes folks do look a bit confused when I'm talking. Like at the church, when the ladies ask me about the new blankets for the bazaar or what time choir practice is. I always mean to give them a straight answer, I really do. But then I start talking, and my words just sort of run off on their own... I'll start talking about the green beans I'm canning, and then that reminds me of a hymn we sang last Easter, and before I know it I'm going on about how my yellow roses are doing this year. And they just stare at me, like they can't quite find what I'm saying in all of that. My roses really are lovely this year, though. I put down some crushed eggshells around the roots, just like my mother used to do. She always saved her eggshells in a little tin cup on the windowsill. It was a blue tin cup, the kind that had those little white speckles all over it. We used to take those cups camping when I was a little girl... but yes, I suppose people do have a hard time following me sometimes.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, let me think... I'd say it's been about six months now that folks have been mentioning my talking being hard to follow, and noticing me acting a bit odd. Six months... that would mean it started right around the time of the big autumn bake sale at the church. I remember because I made three dozen of my special molasses cookies. You have to use the real dark molasses, none of that light stuff, or they just don't taste right. And a pinch of ginger, that's the secret. My sister Edna, she always tried to make them, but hers always came out flat as a pancake. She never did have the touch for baking, Edna. She was better with sewing. She made me the most beautiful quilt for my wedding, all done in the double wedding ring pattern. It had these lovely little scraps of calico fabric... I think some of them were from our old dresses when we were girls. I still have it folded up at the bottom of my cedar chest. Cedar smells so wonderful, doesn't it? It keeps the moths away, too. I always put a few cedar blocks in with my winter coats when I pack them up for the summer... but yes, it's been about six months since all this started.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF20.2, F06.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, it's nice to meet you too, Doctor. I... I guess I came in because I keep doing these odd things at church lately. \n\nLike, we have a potluck on Sundays. Sister Mary always brings her macaroni salad, though she puts way too much celery in it if you ask me. I used to grow celery when we had the house out on County Road 9, but the soil out there was always too sandy for a good garden. The tomatoes did alright, though. \n\nAnyway, I was standing by the potluck table, and I picked up the big serving spoon for the potatoes, and I just... held it up in the air. I just stood there holding it up and not moving at all. Just froze right there. \n\nAnd then later, I was at the end of the table with the napkins. They were those yellow paper ones. Yellow is such a happy color, it reminds me of the kitchen curtains I bought at Sears back when they still mailed out that big thick catalog... I really miss getting that catalog in the mail. But I was folding the same stack of napkins, just folding them over and over, smoothing them down and folding them again, and all those people were just standing there in line waiting on me to finish so they could get their silverware. I don't really know why I get stuck like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear voices when no one else is there?\n\nPatient: Voices? No, no, I don't hear anything like that. Just the regular sounds, you know? Like the wind chimes on my porch. I have these beautiful glass ones my sister got me when she went down to Florida. Clearwater, I think it was. Or maybe Daytona? She always loved the beach, but I could never stand the sand getting in my shoes. It just ruins a good pair of loafers. I used to have a pair of penny loafers that I wore until the soles practically fell off. They were so comfortable, like walking on clouds. But no... no voices. Just the wind chimes.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times lately when you felt unusually energetic or excited for days at a time?\n\nPatient: Energetic? Oh, heavens no. I don't think I've had that kind of energy since... well, since I was a girl. I remember one summer, I must have been about twelve, and we were staying at my aunt's farm. She had these big, beautiful draft horses. Clydesdales, they were. Such gentle giants, you know? They had those big feathery hooves. I used to brush their coats until they shone like copper pennies. Pennies used to buy you a whole handful of candy down at the mercantile. Peppermint sticks, licorice whips... I always liked the butterscotch discs the best. They came in those little shiny yellow wrappers. So pretty. But no, I wouldn't say I've felt unusually excited or anything like that lately. Just my normal self, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Since you were a child, have social situations usually felt confusing to you?\n\nPatient: Confusing? Oh, I don't know... I've always just been me, you know? I remember when I first started school, it was a little one-room schoolhouse out past the old mill. The teacher, Miss Gable, she had the most beautiful handwriting. Perfect cursive, all those loops and swirls. We used to practice on these little slate boards with chalk that always made my hands feel so dry. I hate that feeling, dry hands. I use this rosewater lotion now, comes in a pink bottle. Smells just like the roses my grandmother grew on her trellis. They were those big climbing roses, the kind that bloom all summer long... But no, I wouldn't say confusing. Just... normal, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent medical illness or brain problem, like a seizure, stroke, or head injury?\n\nPatient: A stroke or a head injury? Oh, goodness no. My head is perfectly fine. I haven't been sick or in a hospital since... well, since my Henry broke his collarbone. That was years and years ago. He was up fixing the roof on that old shed out back. It had those green shingles, you know? The kind that get all thick with moss if you don't scrape them down in the spring. I told him to hire someone to do it, but he was always so stubborn about fixing things himself. Just like his father. His father used to smoke this pipe that smelled just like sweet cherry wood. It would fill up the whole porch. Smelled a bit like cherry pie, actually. I love a good cherry pie, especially if you make the crust from scratch and do the lattice on top. You have to weave the dough strips just right or they sink right down into the fruit while it bakes... But no, no seizures or anything like that for me. Just healthy, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe anyone is trying to harm you or control you?\n\nPatient: Harm me? Oh, heavens no. Why would anyone want to do that? I keep to myself mostly, just me and my little garden out back. I've been trying to get the tomatoes to come in, but the squirrels keep getting to them before they turn red. They're such clever little things, squirrels. They have those bushy tails that twitch when they're excited. I used to watch them chase each other up and down the big oak tree we had in the front yard when I was a girl. That tree must have been a hundred years old. My brother built a treehouse up in the branches. We'd sit up there for hours and eat sandwiches... egg salad, usually. Mother always put a little bit of paprika in her egg salad, gave it such a nice color. I still have her old recipe box, it's made of tin with little painted daisies on the lid. But no, nobody's trying to control me or anything like that. I'm just fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeating other people’s words right after they say them?\n\nPatient: Repeating words? No, I don't think I do that. It sounds a bit like an echo, doesn't it? Like when you stand at the edge of a canyon and shout down into it. We went to the Grand Canyon once, Henry and I, for our twenty-fifth anniversary. It was so hot that day, the sun just beating down on the rocks. The rocks were all these different shades of red and orange, like a painting. I bought a little postcard from the gift shop with a picture of a mule on it. They take people down into the canyon on mules, you know. I'd never ride one, though. Mules can be so stubborn. My uncle Arthur had a mule on his farm back when I was little. He called him Barnaby. Barnaby loved to eat carrots right out of your hand, but you had to keep your fingers flat or he'd nip you. I was always so careful with my hands around him... But no, I don't go around repeating what people say.\n\nDoctor: As a child, did you often do the same movement or routine over and over again?\n\nPatient: Over and over? No, I don't recall doing anything like that. I was just a regular little girl, mostly just running around outside. We did play jump rope a lot, I suppose, if that counts? You have to jump the exact same way every time or the rope catches your ankles. My friend Martha and I would jump rope for hours on the sidewalk until the streetlights came on. Martha had the most beautiful blonde hair, always tied up in two braids with little red ribbons. Her mother made those ribbons from scraps of velvet. Velvet is such a difficult fabric to sew, you know. It slips all over the machine if you aren't careful. I tried making a velvet dress for my baby doll once, but the needle kept jamming. It was a Singer sewing machine, one of those heavy black iron ones with the gold filigree painted on the side. My mother bought it second-hand from a lady down the street who was moving up to Chicago. Chicago always sounded so windy and cold to me, all those tall buildings blocking out the sun... I've never been, myself. But no, no strange routines or movements for me. Just regular playing.\n\nDoctor: When someone tries to move your arms or body while you’re frozen, do you stay in that position?\n\nPatient: Frozen? Oh, goodness, no. I don't freeze up like a statue or anything. And who would be trying to move my arms anyway? It's just me here. The only thing frozen around here is the ground beef I put in the icebox. I always buy the big family pack when it goes on sale at the market. You have to remember to separate it into little portions before you freeze it, though, or it takes absolutely forever to thaw out on the counter. My neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, she just throws the whole big block right into her freezer, paper and all. Then she complains when she can't get her meatloaf made in time for supper. She makes a terribly dry meatloaf anyway, puts way too much onion in it. Onions give me such awful heartburn. I always have to drink a little glass of milk with a spoonful of baking soda stirred in if I have onions. My grandmother taught me that trick. She lived to be ninety-two, bless her heart, and she swore by baking soda for just about everything... But no, I move my own arms just fine. Nobody is posing me like a mannequin.\n\nDoctor: When you get stuck like that, do you stop talking too?\n\nPatient: Stuck? Well, like I just said, I don't get frozen or stuck in the first place. And I certainly don't stop talking. My husband Henry, God rest his soul, he always used to say I could talk the ears off a brass monkey. He had all these funny little sayings like that. His father was in the Merchant Marines, you see, so Henry picked up all sorts of phrases from him. He had this beautiful old compass his father brought back from a trip... maybe it was Nova Scotia? It was solid brass and just so heavy. I used to have to polish it every Sunday before church with that stinky Brasso cream. It always left a terrible black smudge on my good dusting rags. I tried soaking them in bleach, but you have to be so careful with bleach or it eats right through the cotton. I ruined one of my favorite aprons that way, a lovely little yellow gingham one with pockets on the front. I used to keep my clothespins in those pockets when I hung the wash out on the line... But no, I don't stop talking or get stuck like you're saying.\n\nDoctor: Do people ever tell you that your talking jumps from one idea to another in a way that’s hard to follow?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose sometimes folks do look a bit confused when I'm talking. Like at the church, when the ladies ask me about the new blankets for the bazaar or what time choir practice is. I always mean to give them a straight answer, I really do. But then I start talking, and my words just sort of run off on their own... I'll start talking about the green beans I'm canning, and then that reminds me of a hymn we sang last Easter, and before I know it I'm going on about how my yellow roses are doing this year. And they just stare at me, like they can't quite find what I'm saying in all of that. My roses really are lovely this year, though. I put down some crushed eggshells around the roots, just like my mother used to do. She always saved her eggshells in a little tin cup on the windowsill. It was a blue tin cup, the kind that had those little white speckles all over it. We used to take those cups camping when I was a little girl... but yes, I suppose people do have a hard time following me sometimes.\n\nDoctor: About how long have your speech and these getting-stuck episodes been going on?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think... I'd say it's been about six months now that folks have been mentioning my talking being hard to follow, and noticing me acting a bit odd. Six months... that would mean it started right around the time of the big autumn bake sale at the church. I remember because I made three dozen of my special molasses cookies. You have to use the real dark molasses, none of that light stuff, or they just don't taste right. And a pinch of ginger, that's the secret. My sister Edna, she always tried to make them, but hers always came out flat as a pancake. She never did have the touch for baking, Edna. She was better with sewing. She made me the most beautiful quilt for my wedding, all done in the double wedding ring pattern. It had these lovely little scraps of calico fabric... I think some of them were from our old dresses when we were girls. I still have it folded up at the bottom of my cedar chest. Cedar smells so wonderful, doesn't it? It keeps the moths away, too. I always put a few cedar blocks in with my winter coats when I pack them up for the summer... but yes, it's been about six months since all this started.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nWell, it's nice to meet you too, Doctor. I... I guess I came in because I keep doing these odd things at church lately. \n\nLike, we have a potluck on Sundays. Sister Mary always brings her macaroni salad, though she puts way too much celery in it if you ask me. I used to grow celery when we had the house out on County Road 9, but the soil out there was always too sandy for a good garden. The tomatoes did alright, though. \n\nAnyway, I was standing by the potluck table, and I picked up the big serving spoon for the potatoes, and I just... held it up in the air. I just stood there holding it up and not moving at all. Just froze right there. \n\nAnd then later, I was at the end of the table with the napkins. They were those yellow paper ones. Yellow is such a happy color, it reminds me of the kitchen curtains I bought at Sears back when they still mailed out that big thick catalog... I really miss getting that catalog in the mail. But I was folding the same stack of napkins, just folding them over and over, smoothing them down and folding them again, and all those people were just standing there in line waiting on me to finish so they could get their silverware. I don't really know why I get stuck like that.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Auditory Hallucinations, Visual Hallucinations, Tactile Hallucinations, Olfactory Hallucinations, Gustatory Hallucinations\n[S021] Disorganized_Thinking_Speech: Symptoms where speech or thought appears scattered and difficult to follow due to a lack of logical or consistent flow.  Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.  Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nEnergetic? Oh, heavens no. I don't think I've had that kind of energy since... well, since I was a girl. I remember one summer, I must have been about twelve, and we were staying at my aunt's farm. She had these big, beautiful draft horses. Clydesdales, they were. Such gentle giants, you know? They had those big feathery hooves. I used to brush their coats until they shone like copper pennies. Pennies used to buy you a whole handful of candy down at the mercantile. Peppermint sticks, licorice whips... I always liked the butterscotch discs the best. They came in those little shiny yellow wrappers. So pretty. But no, I wouldn't say I've felt unusually excited or anything like that lately. Just my normal self, I suppose.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"

Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.
